========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.

And I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.

With the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.

It’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.

At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.

It’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying small clinical imperfections as if they prove I’m a dangerous doctor.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll read the same lab result or inbox message five times and still feel unsure what I just read, so even simple admin decisions can take me an embarrassing amount of time.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to kill myself, but I catch myself thinking that it might be better if I didn’t wake up, especially after a day when I’m convinced I’ve let patients down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: Before this depressive period after the clinic award, I had never heard patient voices or been convinced I had secretly harmed people despite evidence. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.

Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.

So yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.

It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.

But I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.

And socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.

So… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.

not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying small clinical imperfections as if they prove I’m a dangerous doctor.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll read the same lab result or inbox message five times and still feel unsure what I just read, so even simple admin decisions can take me an embarrassing amount of time.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to kill myself, but I catch myself thinking that it might be better if I didn’t wake up, especially after a day when I’m convinced I’ve let patients down.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: Before this depressive period after the clinic award, I had never heard patient voices or been convinced I had secretly harmed people despite evidence. The voices and fixed guilty beliefs only show up when I’m in this low, guilty state.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with non-urgent administrative work and I avoid social plans because I don’t trust my judgment or energy. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.

But it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.

So I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.

But the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.

And relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.

I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.

It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.

And now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.

So yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F48.8", "F41.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: Even when I review the chart and there’s no evidence of a bad outcome, I keep believing that some routine delay or imperfect message from me has quietly harmed a patient and that everyone will eventually discover it.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: When I’m alone after clinic or trying to fall asleep, I sometimes hear a patient’s voice as clearly as if they were in the room saying, 'You missed it,' and I’ll check the hallway or my phone even though nothing is there.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to plan weekend hikes, sunrise photos, and big Indonesian dinners for friends, but for months now those things feel like obligations I can’t make myself care about.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: At work I move so slowly between rooms and sit staring at the EHR before clicking anything that nurses have started gently asking if I’m okay.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.

But it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.

And outside work, I keep avoiding plans. Even simple ones. I’ll think, “Do I actually have the energy to go and be normal?” and then I just don’t commit. Which is funny, because I used to be the one chasing people to make plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.

Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.

So… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.

I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.\n\nBut it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in that way. It’s not like I believe if I replay it enough, then something bad won’t happen.

It’s more like my brain gets stuck on it and keeps circling back, even when I know it’s not useful. Like when you’re trying to remember if you locked the door, except… not exactly, because at work there are real consequences, so it feels harder to just let it go. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.

But no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.
