---------- turn 17 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.\n\nBut it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. Even simple ones. I’ll think, “Do I actually have the energy to go and be normal?” and then I just don’t commit. Which is funny, because I used to be the one chasing people to make plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.\n\nSometimes I’ll think about checking something again, or I’ll mentally go back over what I said, but then I also do that as a doctor anyway, so it’s hard to know where the line is. Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.\n\nSo… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay these clinical imperfections over and over to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. It’s not like I believe if I replay it enough, then something bad won’t happen.\n\nIt’s more like my brain gets stuck on it and keeps circling back, even when I know it’s not useful. Like when you’re trying to remember if you locked the door, except… not exactly, because at work there are real consequences, so it feels harder to just let it go. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F48.8", "F41.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yes… I think it is, although not in the dramatic way that sounds.

I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the clinic day. So if someone just looked at my schedule, they’d probably think I’m functioning fine. But the extra bits around it—the admin, the non-urgent stuff, emails or forms or following up things that don’t have to be done immediately—I’ve started asking associates to help with those more than I used to.

It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more that I get stuck not trusting my judgment, and then something simple takes ages. And then I feel worse because I know I’m being less reliable.

With colleagues, I don’t think I’m having conflict exactly. But I’m probably less… connected. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.\n\nSometimes I’ll think about checking something again, or I’ll mentally go back over what I said, but then I also do that as a doctor anyway, so it’s hard to know where the line is. Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.\n\nSo… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay these clinical imperfections over and over to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. It’s not like I believe if I replay it enough, then something bad won’t happen.\n\nIt’s more like my brain gets stuck on it and keeps circling back, even when I know it’s not useful. Like when you’re trying to remember if you locked the door, except… not exactly, because at work there are real consequences, so it feels harder to just let it go. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and feelings are impacting your ability to perform your job as a doctor or affecting your relationships with colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, although not in the dramatic way that sounds.\n\nI’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the clinic day. So if someone just looked at my schedule, they’d probably think I’m functioning fine. But the extra bits around it—the admin, the non-urgent stuff, emails or forms or following up things that don’t have to be done immediately—I’ve started asking associates to help with those more than I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more that I get stuck not trusting my judgment, and then something simple takes ages. And then I feel worse because I know I’m being less reliable.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think I’m having conflict exactly. But I’m probably less… connected. I avoid social plans, even casual ones, because I don’t know if I’ll have the energy to show up properly or be normal company. I used to be better about that. So yes, it’s affecting work a bit, and it’s definitely affecting how present I am with people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and feelings are impacting your ability to perform your job as a doctor or affecting your relationships with colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, although not in the dramatic way that sounds.\n\nI’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the clinic day. So if someone just looked at my schedule, they’d probably think I’m functioning fine. But the extra bits around it—the admin, the non-urgent stuff, emails or forms or following up things that don’t have to be done immediately—I’ve started asking associates to help with those more than I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more that I get stuck not trusting my judgment, and then something simple takes ages. And then I feel worse because I know I’m being less reliable.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think I’m having conflict exactly. But I’m probably less… connected. I avoid social plans, even casual ones, because I don’t know if I’ll have the energy to show up properly or be normal company. I used to be better about that. So yes, it’s affecting work a bit, and it’s definitely affecting how present I am with people.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function in your daily life or relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with non-urgent administrative work and I avoid social plans because I don’t trust my judgment or energy. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. I’m still getting up, going to clinic, seeing patients. On paper it probably looks like I’m doing okay.

But it is interfering, yeah. I’ve had to lean on associates more for the non-urgent admin things—forms, emails, the sort of tasks I used to just get through. Now I second-guess myself or feel like I can’t quite trust my judgment, and then it takes too long or I avoid it.

And outside work, I’m pulling back. I’ll say no to social plans because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy to be decent company. Which sounds silly when I say it, because it’s just dinner or drinks, not some major responsibility… but it feels like too much. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I feel at work and how connected I am with people.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. At work, I keep going over tiny things from clinic—like something I said imperfectly, or a decision that wasn’t perfectly clean—and it feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow, like I shouldn’t be allowed to be in that role.\n\nAnd I know everyone makes small adjustments or has imperfect moments, I’d tell a colleague that in a second, but when it’s me it just… it sticks. I replay it over and over. Even things that probably no one else noticed. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not replaying these moments from work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way, I guess. It’s hard to separate “anxious” from just being kind of keyed into whether I’ve messed something up.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or walking somewhere, I can be okay for a bit, and then my mind sort of… circles back. But when it’s not doing that, I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly on edge. I may be missing it, though. I tend to notice things more when they’re already attached to work somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself replaying or ruminating on things that aren't related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it circles back to work. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.\n\nBut it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. Even simple ones. I’ll think, “Do I actually have the energy to go and be normal?” and then I just don’t commit. Which is funny, because I used to be the one chasing people to make plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.\n\nSometimes I’ll think about checking something again, or I’ll mentally go back over what I said, but then I also do that as a doctor anyway, so it’s hard to know where the line is. Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.\n\nSo… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay these clinical imperfections over and over to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. It’s not like I believe if I replay it enough, then something bad won’t happen.\n\nIt’s more like my brain gets stuck on it and keeps circling back, even when I know it’s not useful. Like when you’re trying to remember if you locked the door, except… not exactly, because at work there are real consequences, so it feels harder to just let it go. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and feelings are impacting your ability to perform your job as a doctor or affecting your relationships with colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, although not in the dramatic way that sounds.\n\nI’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the clinic day. So if someone just looked at my schedule, they’d probably think I’m functioning fine. But the extra bits around it—the admin, the non-urgent stuff, emails or forms or following up things that don’t have to be done immediately—I’ve started asking associates to help with those more than I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more that I get stuck not trusting my judgment, and then something simple takes ages. And then I feel worse because I know I’m being less reliable.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think I’m having conflict exactly. But I’m probably less… connected. I’m still getting up, going to clinic, seeing patients. On paper it probably looks like I’m doing okay.\n\nBut it is interfering, yeah. I’ve had to lean on associates more for the non-urgent admin things—forms, emails, the sort of tasks I used to just get through. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.\n\nBut it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. Even simple ones. I’ll think, “Do I actually have the energy to go and be normal?” and then I just don’t commit. Which is funny, because I used to be the one chasing people to make plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.\n\nSometimes I’ll think about checking something again, or I’ll mentally go back over what I said, but then I also do that as a doctor anyway, so it’s hard to know where the line is. Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.\n\nSo… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay these clinical imperfections over and over to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. Yes… they do come in when I’m not trying to think about them.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F48.8, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I mean, I can ruminate about other things in the ordinary way—like whether I sounded weird in a text or forgot to reply to someone—but it doesn’t have the same… weight.\n\nWith the clinical stuff, even tiny imperfections start to feel like evidence. Like if I phrased something badly or missed a cleaner way to handle something, I’ll replay it as if it proves I’m not safe to trust. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because I know colleagues make small mistakes and move on, and I’d never judge them this harshly. But for me it becomes this whole thing about whether I deserve to be in the role at all.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like you're similarly replaying imperfections or mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I can overthink normal things, like whether I was awkward at dinner or something, but it doesn’t become… this evidence file against me.\n\nIt’s mostly work. The clinical stuff. I’ll replay some small imperfect moment, like a phrasing or a judgment call, and it starts feeling like proof that I’m dangerous, like I shouldn’t be trusted with patients. Which is strange because, you know, if a colleague told me the same thing, I’d probably reassure them immediately. But when it’s me, it feels like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and replays affecting your ability to do things you normally enjoy outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I sit there and decide not to enjoy things. It’s more that the things I used to naturally plan just feel… flat, or like admin.\n\nI used to be the person who’d map out a weekend hike, check the sunrise time, bring the camera, make too much coffee. Or I’d do these big Indonesian dinners for friends—like, spend half the day getting the sambal right, which my aunt would probably still say was wrong, but anyway. Now even thinking about organizing that feels like another obligation. I can’t really make myself care about it the way I used to. It’s been like that for months, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart every second, but it sits there and makes everything feel heavy.\n\nIt’s like I’m here because I feel like I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. I keep replaying these small clinical imperfections—things that might sound minor if I explained them on paper—and they start to feel like proof that I’m a dangerous doctor. And then I’ll get distracted by stupid details, like remembering exactly where I was standing when I said something, or what the lighting was like in the room, which is probably irrelevant, but my brain treats it like it matters.\n\nSo… mild on the outside maybe, but inside it’s upsetting. It makes me feel guilty, like I’ve fooled people into thinking I’m safe to trust.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of not deserving trust or the replaying of clinical imperfections affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I’m still going to clinic, I’m not just disappearing or anything, but I’ve had to ask people for help with some of the non-urgent admin stuff because I just don’t trust myself to handle it cleanly, or I run out of steam.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve been avoiding plans more. Friends will suggest dinner or something, and I’ll think, “I should go,” and then it becomes this whole calculation of whether I’ll be present enough or whether I’ll say something off. Which is silly, maybe. My cousin used to joke that I could turn a coffee into a committee meeting, and now even replying to a text feels like… I don’t know, like I need clearance.\n\nSo yes, I guess it’s affecting how reliable I am at work, and I’m definitely less connected to people than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get anxious, obviously, and sometimes it kind of spikes when I’m thinking too much about something… but “intense fear that peaks within minutes” sounds more dramatic than what I’ve noticed.\n\nI did have this one time in the grocery store where I suddenly felt like everyone was moving too slowly, which is ridiculous because it was just a Tuesday and the self-checkout was broken—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I don’t think I’ve had episodes like that in the way you mean. At least, not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or tasks that you're avoiding because of these replayed thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still going to clinic. I’m not avoiding that exactly, which I keep telling myself counts for something.\n\nBut I have been avoiding some of the non-urgent administrative things—forms, follow-ups, the sort of inbox stuff that shouldn’t feel morally loaded but somehow does. I’ve asked associates for help with some of it because I start second-guessing whether I’ll miss something or word something badly. It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the task is hard.\n\nAnd socially, yes. I’ve been dodging plans more. Dinner, coffee, even just replying to people sometimes. I’ll think, “If I’m this tired or this unsure of myself, maybe I shouldn’t be around people,” which sounds overly dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nSo… yes. I’m avoiding things, mostly the tasks where I don’t trust my judgment or my energy. It’s starting to make me less reliable at work and more disconnected outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Not really time off, no. I’m still showing up to clinic, which I keep sort of clinging to as proof that I’m functioning.\n\nBut it’s not… normal functioning, I guess. I’ve had to lean on associates for some non-urgent admin work because I get stuck second-guessing myself or I just don’t have the energy to push through it. It’s funny, admin used to annoy me in the normal way—everyone complains about forms—but now it feels like this whole test of whether I can be trusted with small things.\n\nSo I haven’t taken official time off, but it is affecting how reliable I am. And outside work I’m avoiding people more, canceling or not making plans, because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy or judgment to be decent company.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to make these replayed thoughts or feelings of not deserving trust go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing, no. Not like… a ritual or something I have to do to make it stop.\n\nMostly I just try to push it away or distract myself, or I’ll reread something I wrote and then somehow end up checking an email three times, but that feels more like indecision than a “thing I have to do.” Sorry, I’m splitting hairs.\n\nI guess the short answer is: I haven’t noticed any particular action that reliably makes the thoughts go away. They just sort of fade or don’t, depending on the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall performance or relationships at work due to these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I haven’t stopped going to clinic or anything. I’m still there, still seeing patients.\n\nBut the edges are fraying, I guess. The non-urgent admin stuff has gotten harder than it should be, and I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I normally would just handle myself. It’s not even that the work is complicated, it’s more that I don’t trust my judgment, or I feel too drained to be sure I’m doing it right.\n\nAnd relationships… at work I don’t think I’m snapping at people or anything, but I’m probably less reliable. Outside work I’ve been avoiding plans more—coffee, dinner, even simple things—because I keep thinking I won’t have the energy to show up properly. So yes, it’s started to affect both, just kind of quietly.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “significantly” makes me want to argue with the word, because I’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the basic things. So part of me wants to say no.\n\nBut yes, it is interfering. I’ve had to ask for help with admin things that aren’t urgent, which feels embarrassing because it’s the sort of stuff I used to just knock out between patients or after clinic. Now I get stuck wondering if I’m missing something or if I can trust my own judgment.\n\nAnd socially, I’m pulling back. I’ll avoid making plans or I’ll think about canceling because I don’t trust that I’ll have enough energy to be… I don’t know, presentable as a person. It’s not like I’ve cut everyone off completely, but I’m definitely less connected than I was. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I am at work and how much I’m showing up for people outside of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust making it harder for you to enjoy time with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I used to be the person who organized things, you know? Weekend hikes, getting up stupidly early to take sunrise photos, making these big Indonesian dinners for friends—too much food, always, because I can’t estimate rice properly.\n\nAnd now, for months, those things just feel like… tasks. Obligations. Like I know I should want to do them, and I know they used to make me happy, but I can’t really make myself care enough to plan them or enjoy them the same way.\n\nSo yes, time with friends and family is harder to enjoy. Even when I’m there, part of me is just trying to get through it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust are controlling your life or making it hard for you to function normally?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s controlling my whole life… I mean, I still get up, I still go to clinic, I’m still seeing patients. So in that sense I can function.\n\nBut it’s making normal things harder than they should be. The non-urgent admin work especially—I’ve had to ask associates to help with things I would normally just deal with myself, because I get stuck doubting whether I’m doing it right or whether I can trust my judgment.\n\nAnd outside work, I keep avoiding plans. Even simple ones. I’ll think, “Do I actually have the energy to go and be normal?” and then I just don’t commit. Which is funny, because I used to be the one chasing people to make plans. So… no, it’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but yes, it’s definitely started to shape what I do and how reliable I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it an overwhelming urge, no. I mean, I replay things in my head and get caught on little details, but I don’t think I have a… specific ritual or action I feel forced to do afterward.\n\nSometimes I’ll think about checking something again, or I’ll mentally go back over what I said, but then I also do that as a doctor anyway, so it’s hard to know where the line is. Like, everyone replays awkward conversations, right? Or maybe not everyone. I remember once after a consult I spent ages thinking about whether my tone was too abrupt, and then somehow that turned into remembering a completely unrelated teaching session from residency where someone got criticized for documentation style, which is ridiculous because that was years ago.\n\nSo… I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed a repeated action or mental act that I feel driven to do in a fixed way. It’s more that I get stuck thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay these clinical imperfections over and over to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. It’s not like I believe if I replay it enough, then something bad won’t happen.\n\nIt’s more like my brain gets stuck on it and keeps circling back, even when I know it’s not useful. Like when you’re trying to remember if you locked the door, except… not exactly, because at work there are real consequences, so it feels harder to just let it go. Sorry, that’s probably not answering cleanly.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed that I’m replaying things as a way to prevent something specific from happening. It’s more just… rumination, I guess. Getting caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and feelings are impacting your ability to perform your job as a doctor or affecting your relationships with colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, although not in the dramatic way that sounds.\n\nI’m still going in, still seeing patients, still doing the clinic day. So if someone just looked at my schedule, they’d probably think I’m functioning fine. But the extra bits around it—the admin, the non-urgent stuff, emails or forms or following up things that don’t have to be done immediately—I’ve started asking associates to help with those more than I used to.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more that I get stuck not trusting my judgment, and then something simple takes ages. And then I feel worse because I know I’m being less reliable.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think I’m having conflict exactly. But I’m probably less… connected. I avoid social plans, even casual ones, because I don’t know if I’ll have the energy to show up properly or be normal company. I used to be better about that. So yes, it’s affecting work a bit, and it’s definitely affecting how present I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings of not deserving trust causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. I’m still getting up, going to clinic, seeing patients. On paper it probably looks like I’m doing okay.\n\nBut it is interfering, yeah. I’ve had to lean on associates more for the non-urgent admin things—forms, emails, the sort of tasks I used to just get through. Now I second-guess myself or feel like I can’t quite trust my judgment, and then it takes too long or I avoid it.\n\nAnd outside work, I’m pulling back. I’ll say no to social plans because I don’t trust that I’ll have the energy to be decent company. Which sounds silly when I say it, because it’s just dinner or drinks, not some major responsibility… but it feels like too much. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I feel at work and how connected I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections are intrusive, meaning they enter your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do come in when I’m not trying to think about them.\n\nI’ll be doing something else, or trying to move on to the next patient or even just make coffee, and suddenly I’m back on some small thing I said in clinic, or something I could’ve documented better. And it’s not even always a clear mistake, just an imperfection, but my mind treats it like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like people are trusting me with something I don’t deserve.\n\nSorry, I know you asked whether they’re intrusive. Yes, I’d say they are. I don’t sit down and choose to replay them. They just sort of barge in, and then I’m stuck with this feeling that maybe I’m a dangerous doctor.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So yes, it’s affecting how reliable I feel at work and how connected I am with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these replayed thoughts about clinical imperfections are intrusive, meaning they enter your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do come in when I’m not trying to think about them.\n\nI’ll be doing something else, or trying to move on to the next patient or even just make coffee, and suddenly I’m back on some small thing I said in clinic, or something I could’ve documented better. And it’s not even always a clear mistake, just an imperfection, but my mind treats it like evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted. Like people are trusting me with something I don’t deserve.\n\nSorry, I know you asked whether they’re intrusive. Yes, I’d say they are. I don’t sit down and choose to replay them. They just sort of barge in, and then I’m stuck with this feeling that maybe I’m a dangerous doctor."
